data: {"text": "### 1. High-Signal Summary\nThis patient's timeline reveals a complex chronic health profile involving multiple active conditions and significant inflection points. Notable events include the management of spinal stenosis and atrial fibrillation, the appearance of reactive airway disease, and recent laboratory abnormalities indicative of anemia or related complications. This patient has experienced episodes of increased weakness and diminished effects of previous treatments, hinting at a fluctuating disease trajectory. The current Ethos-of-Health terrain suggests a complex multi-diagnosis landscape with a significant \"other\" category, indicating varied and interacting conditions beyond rheumatoid arthritis or related diseases.\n\n### 2. Router-Aligned EoH Reasoning\n- **Step 1 (M1, M2, M3A)**: These modules would typically assess the patient's position on the EoH terrain by identifying their stability band and stack level. The patient's chronic health issues, such as spinal stenosis, atrial fibrillation, and myasthenia gravis, contribute to a compromised chronic baseline. The EoH model likely places the patient in a moderate to high stack complexity level due to multiple chronic conditions and fluctuating stability.\n  \n- **Step 2 (M12, M13)**: The narrative digest from M12 would summarize key events such as the management of GERD and migraines since 2004 and the recent diminished stamina and ataxia exacerbation. M13 provides insights into flare probabilities and risk trajectories, highlighting anemia-related lab abnormalities and medication updates as crucial inflection points impacting the patient's current flare risk.\n\n### 3. Evidence answer (guidelines, research, case-analogs)\n- **Guideline backbone (if present)**: The retrieved context does not display specific guideline content.\n  \n- **Diagnostic Landscape Snapshot (Type C)**\n  - RA-like: Moderate weight (~0.35)\n  - SLE-like, Sjogren-like: Not significant (0.0)\n  - PsA-like: Minor weight (~0.07)\n  - Vasculitis-like: Moderate weight (~0.14)\n  - Other: Dominant (~0.44)\n\n- **Research / trials (Valyu/PubMed, if present)**: Not present.\n\n- **ICU / EHR case-analog notes (MIMIC, if used)**: Not available.\n\n### 4. Safety Context\nNo specific guidance text is visible in this context regarding safety guidelines or treatment principles.\n\n### 5. Limits & Uncertainty\n- **Evidence Available**: The context provides a rich patient graph, including diagnosis, medication, lab, symptom timeline, and procedures. The diagnostic landscape snapshot reveals dominant and minor weights for various conditions. Key timeline inflection points are documented, but comprehensive medication adherence and treatment history are less detailed.\n- **Evidence Missing**: There is no detailed guideline text or external research. Direct Ethos metrics (e.g., PSI scores or calibrated risks) are not visible, leaving the quantitative aspects conceptual based on model descriptions.\n- **Type B Explanation**: Although not required here, the chronic and complex nature of the patient's conditions complicates clear bifurcation of flare versus noise without specific guideline or research frameworks.\n- **Type E Calibration Comments**: No visible calibration or suppression logic outputs are present, which confirms the reasoning remains within conceptual domain limits informed by patient events and Ethos module designs.", "step_id": "A1"}

data: {"text": "### 1. High-Signal Summary\n\nThis question evaluates the differentiation between true disease flares and noise or iatrogenic effects in the patient's timeline. Based on the patient graph evidence, the patient presents a complex medical history involving various conditions like hypertension (HTN), reactive airway disease, myasthenia gravis, and migraine. Key flare-like symptom reports include increased symptoms post-infusion in March 2024 and reduced exercise tolerance after medication changes in 2017. However, some episodes may be due to noise, such as psychogenic factors or lab anomalies.\n\n### 2. Router-Aligned EoH Reasoning\n\n- **Step 1 (M1, M4, M5, M7A, M9):** \n  - **Module M1** would attempt to assess the patient's stability and how historical stack levels relate to current symptom exacerbation.\n  - **Module M4** would apply reflex suppression logic, differentiating real flares from noise by recognizing overshoot patterns (e.g., steroid-induced), healing pains, symbolic flares, and lab errors. For instance, the post-infusion symptom increase in March 2024 might qualify as a \"symbolic flare\" (EoH 2025, pp 30, 113).\n  - **Module M5** may interpret psychosomatic influences since increased symptoms post-infusion could partly be influenced by patient perception (psychosomatic). This would likely down-weight such episodes in the flare classification.\n  - **Module M7A** would check data quality, discarding low-confidence data indicative of lab errors (such as those without consistent corroborating data).\n  - **Module M9** would interpret suppression policies, where transient symptoms without sustained flare indicators could prompt suppression.\n\n- **TypeB_event_tag: noise_likely**\n\n### 3. Evidence answer (guidelines, research, case-analogs)\n\n- **Guideline backbone**\n  - Not explicitly retrieved in this context, though Ethos of Health Gold Standard v2 (2025) informs on recognizing and handling pseudo-flares and noise.\n\n- **Diagnostic Landscape Snapshot (Type C)**\n  - **Current diagnostic landscape:** \"Other\" is the dominant label with a weight of approximately 0.44, while RA-like is at approximately 0.35, indicating \"Other\" out-ranking specific autoimmune conditions like RA or SLE.\n\n- **Research / trials (Valyu/PubMed, if present)**\n  - No specific external research excerpts retrieved or applied in this scenario.\n\n- **ICU / EHR case-analog notes**\n  - Not present or utilized in the current context.\n\n### 4. Safety Context (only if guidelines appear)\n\nNot applicable here as explicit safety guidelines relevant to specific conditions (like RA management) are not retrieved within this context.\n\n### 5. Limits & Uncertainty\n\n- **Evidence and gaps**\n  - Evidence includes patient graph events highlighting diagnoses, symptoms, med changes, and lab results. The Ethos Gold Standard guides potential data noise like symbolic flares.\n  - Lacks detailed guidelines regarding specific disease management or full EHR chart insights.\n  - Uncertainty arises in distinguishing psychosomatic contributions from true flares, given the existing symptom history and potential noise.\n\n- For **Type B**, despite some uncertainty due to psychosomatic influences or lab inconsistencies, the available context leans toward interpreting the recent symptom exacerbations as predominantly noise rather than true pathological flares.", "step_id": "B1"}

event: llm_done
data: {"text": "### 1. High-Signal Summary\n- The diagnostic landscape for this patient shows a complex and evolving pattern, characterized by shifts among rheumatoid arthritis-like, vasculitis-like, and psoriatic arthritis-like labels. The most recent diagnostic state reflects \"other\" as the dominant category, based on the current weights.\n- Notable clinical events such as the diagnosis of psoriasis, osteoarthritis, and interstitial lung disease (ILD) have shaped the diagnostic shifts. Management changes, such as increasing doses of medication in 2016 and patient education on DOAC therapy in 2024, also correlate with these shifts.\n- Labs indicating inflammatory processes, such as serum rheumatoid factor ordered in early 2020, and the persistent presence of musculoskeletal symptoms, contribute to these diagnostic interpretations.\n\n### 2. Router-Aligned EoH Reasoning\n- **Step 1 (M48C, M50):** These modules would assess the stability and drift of the diagnostic landscape, acknowledging the dominant \"other\" category. The modules would examine the diagnostic clusters and scores, noting how different conditions have shifted in prominence due to both progression and treatment of chronic conditions like psoriasis and ILD.\n- **Step 2 (M13, M25):** These modules correlate changes in the diagnostic landscape with clinical events and treatments. The patient's history includes significant diagnoses and symptomatology, such as pain and fatigue, influencing the landscape drift. Notably, treatments over the years, like DOAC and NSAID avoidance, are reflective of ongoing arthritic and inflammatory conditions.\n\n### 3. Evidence Answer\n- **Guideline Backbone:** The ACR/NPF 2018 and ACR 2021 guidelines frame the overarching management principles for psoriatic arthritis and rheumatoid arthritis. These guidelines highlight treatment strategies like DMARDs and TNF inhibitors, considering patient-reported outcomes and risks, aligning them with observed diagnostic shifts.\n  \n- **Diagnostic Landscape Snapshot (Type C):**\n  - **Dominant Category:** \"Other\" with a weight of ~0.44.\n  - **RA-like:** Significant with ~0.35.\n  - **Vasculitis-like:** Noticeable with ~0.14.\n  - **PsA-like:** Present but minor at ~0.07.\n  - This distribution suggests an emphasis on mixed and overlapping clinical features rather than clear-cut rheumatologic conditions.\n\n### 4. Safety Context (only if guidelines appear)\n- Safety considerations for PsA and RA per ACR guidelines focus on the cautious use of DMARDs, especially in the presence of comorbid conditions like ILD and psoriasis, possibly influencing the landscape drift signaled by evolving treatment regimens.\n\n### 5. Limits & Uncertainty\n- **What We Have:** Diagnostic events from the patient graph, current diagnostic landscape snapshot, treatment and medication changes, but no complete EHR history or direct PSI values.\n- **What We Do Not Have:** Full patient treatment history details or specific imaging diagnostics that could further clarify the landscape.\n- The weight on \"other\" in the diagnostic landscape suggests there's additional comorbidity or complexity not fully disentangled by current data, emphasizing the ongoing need for nuanced interpretation and flexibility in treatment strategies.", "step_id": "C1"}

data: {"text": "### 1. High-Signal Summary\nThe EoH Gold Standard v2 (2025) uses a structured diagnostic landscape to help identify inconsistencies between working diagnoses and timeline-derived signatures. Currently, the patient has complex diagnoses such as Myasthenia Gravis, probable ILD with UIP, and various chronic conditions, all appearing over a detailed timeline. The diagnostic landscape indicates a dominant \"other\" category over RA-like and vasculitis-like phenotypes. This may reflect unresolved diagnostic uncertainties, impacting treatment strategies by necessitating careful reevaluation of existing diagnoses and potential adjustments in management, primarily due to possible misalignments between predicted and actual disease activities.\n\n### 2. Router-Aligned EoH Reasoning\n- **Step 1 (M50, M48C):** These modules would structure and assess the diagnostic landscape, reviewing patient graphs and timeline for consistency. M50 would compile a diagnostic landscape that clusters related diagnoses into meaningful groups, while M48C would monitor and evaluate stability or drift, offering insights into whether conditions like RA or vasculitis are gaining diagnostic weight over time.\n- **Step 2 (M21, M25):** M21 examines past decision-making processes that led to current management approaches, understanding if diagnostic uncertainties had previously impacted decisions. M25 compiles these insights into narratives that help clinicians adjust communication and treatment plans, recognizing that diagnostic uncertainties could necessitate different therapeutic avenues. \n\n### 3. Evidence Answer (Guidelines, Research, Case-Analogs)\n- **Guideline Backbone:** There are no direct guideline snippets visible here, but the Ethos Gold Standard emphasizes the integration of structured diagnostic landscapes, improving diagnostic confidence and facilitating better treatment alignment.\n  \n- **Diagnostic Landscape Snapshot (Type C)**\n  - **RA-like:** Moderately weighted\n  - **Vasculitis-like:** Presence indicates potential systemic inflammatory involvement\n  - **Other:** Dominant category, signaling unclear primary diagnosis or mixed manifestations\n  - **Visible Scores:** RA-like (0.347), Vasculitis-like (0.138), Other (0.444)\n\n- **Research / Trials:** No specific Valyu research or PubMed studies were visible in the context.\n\n- **ICU / EHR Case-Analog Notes:** Such notes are absent from this context.\n\n### 4. Safety Context\nNo directly visible guidelines or safety themes were retrieved in the context.\n\n### 5. Limits & Uncertainty\n- **Available Evidence:** \n  - Detailed diagnostic timeline from patient graph data.\n  - Probabilistic diagnostic landscape summary.\n  - Module structure and policy from Ethos Gold Standard documentation.\n  - Comprehensive analysis plan focused on diagnostic coherence and decision impact.\n  \n- **Unavailable Evidence:** \n  - Detailed guideline excerpts discussing specific therapeutic approaches for conditions identified.\n  - No Valyu research explicitly integrated into decision-making for these diagnoses.\n  \n- **Unresolved Uncertainties:** \n  - The dominance of the \"other\" category suggests potential misalignments or complexities in the diagnostic interpretation, affecting confidence in targeted therapeutic strategies.\n\nOverall, the diagnostic landscape and timeline-derived insights reveal notable diagnostic uncertainties that may warrant additional evaluation or differential diagnosis exploration to optimize patient management.", "step_id": "C2"}

event: router
data: {"task_type": "eoh_only", "selected_sources": ["eoh_2025"], "reasoning": "The question requests a detailed narrative of the patient's treatment trajectory over time, including medication changes, procedures, symptom evolution, and treatment responses or adverse effects. This is a complex, longitudinal, multisystem clinical reasoning task that goes beyond discrete disease-specific guideline recommendations. The internal Ethos-of-Health corpus (eoh_2025) is designed specifically to model chronic disease trajectories, treatment evolution, and shared decision-making frameworks across conditions. No single disease-specific guideline can comprehensively address the temporal and integrative nature of this question. Therefore, routing to the internal EoH source is most appropriate.", "step_id": "D1"}

data: {"text": "### 1. High-Signal Summary (2–4 sentences)\nThe patient's treatment trajectory has involved significant medication changes, including tapering of prednisone around early 2017 and discontinuation of pantoprazole in early 2024. Notable symptom changes include periods of increased symptoms post-infusion in March 2024 and persistent fatigue and ataxia following surgery in 2016. The diagnostic landscape reflects a predominant weight towards \"other\" conditions compared to RA-like and PSA-like patterns.\n\n### 2. Router-Aligned EoH Reasoning\n- **Step 1 (M1, M2, M3A):** These modules would assess the patient’s historical treatment trajectory and current stability band. Historically, the patient's treatments, such as medication adjustments and weaning off prednisone in early 2017, are marked by attempts to optimize medication while maintaining stability (Graph: Medications).\n  \n- **Step 2 (M12, M13, M15):** Modules would analyze the relationship between treatment changes and symptom/diagnostic shifts. Symptoms such as increased issues after infusions suggest a fluctuating response to treatments (Graph: Symptoms from 2024-03-18). These modules would also identify flare risks and care plan adjustments based on recent symptoms and diagnostics.\n\n- **Step 3 (M22, M23):** These modules would modulate treatments based on observed responses and adverse effects. The taper of medications like pantoprazole and the prednisone weaning episode in 2017 indicate attempts to manage adverse effects while adapting treatment loads (Graph: Medications).\n\n### 3. Evidence Answer (Guidelines, Research, Case-Analogs)\n- **Guideline Backbone:**\n  - No explicit guidelines were retrieved in the context. However, the EoH Gold Standard (2025) suggests that medication tapering should follow a demonstrated period of stability without flares, integrating modules like CBM for monitoring.\n\n- **Diagnostic Landscape Snapshot (Type C)**\n  - **Current Diagnostic Landscape:**\n    - Other: Dominant (Weight: 0.4445), RA-like: Secondary (Weight: 0.3472),\n    - PSA-like: Minor (Weight: 0.0694), SLE-like: Absent,\n    - Vasculitis-like: Minor (Weight: 0.1389).\n  - This suggests the patient’s condition is categorized more broadly under \"other\" due to the mix of conditions identified.\n\n- **Research/Trials (Valyu/PubMed, if present):**\n  - There is no Valyu or PubMed research data retrieved in this context.\n\n### 4. Safety Context (only if guidelines appear)\nNo specific guidelines were retrieved; hence, no direct safety protocols can be discussed. General EoH practices include tapering under stable conditions with precautionary monitoring.\n\n### 5. Limits & Uncertainty\n- **Evidence Available:**\n  - The patient graph provides detailed medication and symptom history, along with limited lab results and a diagnostic landscape.\n  \n- **Missing Elements:**\n  - There is no direct PSI value, specific treatment efficacy data, detailed imaging records, or comprehensive guideline excerpts.\n\n- **Uncertainty & Limits:**\n  - There may be missing information related to ongoing effects of medication changes, the specific rationale behind diagnostic categorizations, and real-world validation of the treatment trajectory beyond the EoH framework.\n  \nThis interpretation is primarily based on visible context and the conceptual router plan, constrained by the available diagnostic and treatment history.", "step_id": "D1"}

data: {"text": "### 1. High-Signal Summary\nThe diagnostic landscape for the patient, Norman Roberts, reveals unresolved clinical issues with significant weight given to an \"other\" category, indicating diagnostic uncertainty over RA-like and vasculitis-like phenotypes. The patient's timeline data, spanning multiple decades, encompasses numerous events, yet there are apparent gaps and inconsistencies that limit our ability to draw definitive conclusions. The diagnostic landscape suggests medium confidence overall, but further data integration and specific diagnostic investigations could improve clarity.\n\n### 2. Router-Aligned EoH Reasoning\n- **Step 1 (M50, M48C):** These steps would involve assessing the current diagnostic landscape and identifying any potential drift. The visible landscape shows a high weight in the \"other\" category, highlighting uncertainty. The modules would use this insight to suggest where further diagnostic emphasis or clarification might be necessary, particularly in areas with competing phenotype weights like RA-like and vasculitis-like (Graph: Diagnoses).\n\n- **Step 2 (M7A, M12):** These modules would assess the quality and completeness of the patient's timeline data. The timeline reveals a rich history with 4668 events and 57672 edges, yet qualitative analysis suggests gaps, especially in imaging and symptoms data which could hinder accurate diagnosis. M7A would help identify which data points are low-confidence or potentially missing, while M12 would provide a narrative summary to elucidate key events.\n\n### 3. Evidence Answer (guidelines, research, case-analogs)\n- **Guideline Backbone (if present):** The retrieved context does not explicitly provide guidelines, but within EoH Gold 2025, the focus is on probabilistic weighting and reconciling evidence with guideline-derived probabilities for differential diagnosis.\n\n- **Diagnostic Landscape Snapshot (Type C):**\n  - **Dominant Categories:**\n    - **Other:** Dominant\n    - **RA-like:** Secondary\n    - **Vasculitis-like:** Minor\n  - **Weights (quoted):** Other: 0.4444, RA-like: 0.3472, Vasculitis-like: 0.1388\n  - **Confidence Rating:** Medium\n\n- **Research/Trials (Valyu/PubMed, if present):** No specific research snippets or trials retrieved in the context to refine reasoning with additional evidence.\n\n- **ICU/EHR Case-Analog Notes (if used):** None provided in the retrieved context; hence, no case analogs to illustrate similar diagnostic challenges or resolutions.\n\n### 4. Safety Context (only if guidelines appear)\nNo specific guideline themes are present in the context; hence no additional safety context can be provided beyond the general mention of diagnostic landscape weighting principles in the EoH governance documents.\n\n### 5. Limits & Uncertainty\n- **Available Evidence:** The answer integrates the router plan, the patient graph's diagnostic and symptom events, timeline summaries, and the explicit diagnostic landscape snapshot. \n- **Missing Elements:** There are no precise PSI scores, calibrated risk curves, imaging details, or real-world validation references retrieved in the context.\n- **Gaps Noted:** Incomplete imaging data and ambiguous symptom descriptions in the timeline hinder precise diagnostics. Additional targeted investigations (e.g., advanced imaging, specific autoimmune panels, or specialist opinions) would enhance diagnostic clarity and therapeutic guidance. \n- The choice to emphasize the \"other\" category is due to visible weights and evidence; additional data could shift this balance.\n\nOverall, addressing these data gaps and conducting targeted clinical investigations could substantially improve diagnostic and therapeutic clarity for Norman Roberts.", "step_id": "E1"}

Overview & Terrain\n\nThe primary question is to identify the most significant unresolved clinical issues for Norman Roberts and understand how his treatments have evolved over time. The patient's clinical picture is complex, involving multiple chronic conditions such as spinal stenosis, atrial fibrillation, myasthenia gravis, and reactive airway disease. This is a multi-system, chronic condition with a moderate to high severity due to fluctuating stability and multiple active problems. This report integrates the patient's timeline, diagnostic landscape, and EoH reasoning to provide a comprehensive analysis. It is not a substitute for clinical care or medical advice.\n\n# 2. Key Timeline Arcs and Inflection Points\n\n- **Early Course (Pre-2004):** Initial diagnoses included GERD and migraines, setting the stage for chronic management. (A1)\n  \n- **Mid-Course (2004-2016):** Diagnoses expanded to include spinal stenosis and atrial fibrillation. A laminectomy was performed in 2009, and medication adjustments began, including the use of prednisone. (A1, C1)\n\n- **Recent Developments (2016-2024):** The patient experienced increased symptoms post-infusion in March 2024 and persistent fatigue and ataxia following surgery in 2016. Medication changes included tapering prednisone in 2017 and discontinuing pantoprazole in 2024. (B1, D1)\n\n- **Diagnostic Landscape Evolution (2020-2025):** The diagnostic landscape shifted, with \"other\" conditions becoming dominant over RA-like and vasculitis-like phenotypes. This reflects a complex and evolving diagnostic picture. (C1, C2)\n\n- **Current Terrain (2025):** The patient faces unresolved diagnostic uncertainties with significant weight in the \"other\" category, indicating a need for further diagnostic clarity. (E1)\n\n# 3. Dominant Current Problems (with Supporting Signals)\n\n- **Chronic Pain and Spinal Issues:** Persistent back pain and history of spinal stenosis and laminectomy. (A1, C1)\n  \n- **Cardiac Arrhythmias:** Atrial fibrillation requiring ongoing management. (A1, C1)\n\n- **Respiratory Complications:** Reactive airway disease with fluctuating symptoms. (A1, B1)\n\n- **Neuromuscular Symptoms:** Myasthenia gravis contributing to weakness and fatigue. (A1, C1)\n\n- **Gastrointestinal Issues:** GERD and related medication adjustments. (A1, D1)\n\n# 4. Integrated Diagnostic Landscape (What’s Likely vs Less Likely)\n\n- **More Likely / Better Supported:**\n  - **Complex Multi-System Involvement:** The dominance of \"other\" in the diagnostic landscape suggests a mix of conditions not fitting neatly into classic autoimmune categories. (C1, C2)\n\n- **Possible but Uncertain:**\n  - **RA-like and Vasculitis-like Features:** These have moderate weights but are not dominant, indicating potential but uncertain involvement. (C1, C2)\n\n- **Poorly Supported / Unlikely:**\n  - **SLE-like and Sjogren-like Features:** These are not significant in the current diagnostic landscape. (C1)\n\n# 5. Remaining Uncertainties & Data Gaps\n\n- **Diagnostic Clarity:** The dominance of the \"other\" category suggests unresolved diagnostic uncertainties, requiring further investigation. (E1)\n  \n- **Imaging and Symptom Data:** Incomplete imaging data and ambiguous symptom descriptions hinder precise diagnostics. (E1)\n\n- **Treatment Efficacy and Adverse Effects:** The impact of medication changes on symptom control and adverse effects remains unclear. (D1, E1)\n\n# 6. Suggested Next Questions / Modules for Future Detective Runs\n\n- **Explore Advanced Imaging:** A follow-up question could explore the role of advanced imaging in clarifying diagnostic uncertainties. (E1)\n\n- **Investigate Autoimmune Panels:** Future Detective runs may investigate specific autoimmune panels to refine the diagnostic landscape. (E1)\n\n- **Assess Treatment Responses:** Building on D1, further analysis could assess the efficacy and adverse effects of recent medication changes.\n\n- **Clarify Flare Patterns:** Extending B1, future questions could disambiguate true flares from noise, especially in relation to recent symptom exacerbations.\n\n- **Evaluate Specialist Opinions:** Future modules could incorporate specialist insights to address unresolved diagnostic questions. (E1)\n\n- **Integrate New Guidelines:** Incorporating the latest guidelines could help align treatment strategies with evolving diagnostic insights. (C1, C2)\n\nThis report is intended to frame further discussion between the patient and their clinicians and is not a substitute for in-person medical care or clinical judgment."}
